| Poke             | Touch with the index fingertip. Out of reach: aim the ray (Vision Pro: look) and make a quick pinch                | Touch with the controller tip, or aim the ray, and pull the trigger | Press: on an object (node, edge, field object, bin, note, the hourglass glass) it opens its readout with its verbs as buttons; on a button or an option it presses or chooses it. The hourglass cap is a button: pressing it freezes (X, or A on the off hand).                                                                                                                                    |
| Speak (optional) | While a text field is open                                                                                         | Same                                                                | Fill that field. With voice off or unavailable, a floating keyboard appears instead.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

Ten inputs, each with one meaning. Grab, release, twist, flip and stretch are physical acts that mean what they mean on a real object; poke is the only "button". A quick pinch is a poke and a held pinch is a grab, so a far grab starts about a fifth of a second after the pinch closes.

**Freeze and undo.** History is the list of freezes, shown as lines of sand in the hourglass.

- **Freeze** (press the hourglass cap, or X): commits the current arrangement as a new line and holds it still. The next change you make starts time again from that state. Saving and leaving VR freeze first, so nothing uncommitted is lost.
- **Undo** (flip the hourglass, or Y) goes back one step. If you have changed anything since the last freeze, a flip discards those changes and returns to the last freeze. If nothing has changed, a flip unfreezes the last freeze: everything it hid comes back as a dimmed preview and time runs, so you can adjust and freeze again.
- **Redo and jump** (poke the hourglass glass): a strip of every freeze unrolls, newest at the top, with undone steps shown pale; poke any line to return to it. A pale line stays until you freeze something new.

### The rack

The rack is an arc of shelves on the dominant side, about 30 cm below the graph and a forearm's length out, at elbow height whether you stand or sit. It follows the torso's turn lazily so it is always one reach away, and a far grab reaches it from anywhere.

| Shelf        | Object                                                                                                                                                                                        | Placed in or near the graph, it ...                                                                                 | Its readout's verbs                                               | In graphty it is ...                                     |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| Magnets      | One per numeric attribute, plus a blank text magnet                                                                                                                                           | Pulls nodes toward itself in proportion to their value (text magnet: pulls nodes whose name matches its text)       | Strength, Reverse, Edit text, Remove                              | A layout by attribute; a search                          |
| Wells        | One per algorithm family (centrality, community, paths, components, clustering, flow, link prediction, traversal); the label shows the member it will run, PageRank by default for centrality | Runs that algorithm and pulls nodes down its funnel by score; nodes whose score clears the rim fall in and are kept | Algorithm, Options, Strength, Rim value, Reverse, Ranking, Remove | A run and its result; with the rim raised, a filter step |
| Lamps        | One per numeric attribute                                                                                                                                                                     | Heats the nodes within its cone: color and size by value                                                            | Strength (size range), Palette, Order among lamps, Remove         | A style layer                                            |
| Bins         | Blank, any number                                                                                                                                                                             | Catches what is thrown or carried into it: nodes become a group, field objects become a recipe                      | Name, Members, Pour out, Remove                                   | A group (selection); a recipe                            |
| Fences       | Blank panels                                                                                                                                                                                  | Blocks every law from reaching past it                                                                              | Length, Remove                                                    | The scope of a law or a style layer                      |
| Note slips   | A pad                                                                                                                                                                                         | Sticks to whatever it is dropped onto                                                                               | Edit text, Remove                                                 | A note                                                   |
| Specimen jar | One, at the rack's end                                                                                                                                                                        | Pressing its lid saves                                                                                              | Name, Save, Freezes held                                          | The project                                              |
| Hourglass    | On a pivot at the rack's near end, fist size; X/Y on controllers                                                                                                                              | Cap press freezes, turning it over undoes, poking the glass shows history                                           | Freeze, Undo, Redo, history list                                  | History                                                  |
| Drawer       | At the rack's end; grab its handle and pull                                                                                                                                                   | Lists every magnet, well and lamp the catalog has, grouped by family, with a search field                           | --                                                                | The full catalog                                         |

Nodes and edges have readouts too: a node lists its name, attributes, scores and the laws moving it, with Annotate and Add to bin; an edge lists its endpoints and attributes, with Annotate. Every verb that needs two hands (flip, stretch) is also a button in the object's readout, so everything can be done with one hand.

When an algorithm runs, its score is a new attribute: a matching magnet and lamp appear on the rack and glow once.

### The journey

**1. Enter**

- **What you do:** On the 2D page, press Enter VR.
- **What you see:** The 16 Florentine families float at chest height about 75 cm ahead, still settling under the default force layout. When they come to rest, the hourglass at the rack's near end fills one line of sand: the opening state is frozen as the first entry in history, so there is always something to go back to. The rack sits low on your dominant side.
- **Controls:** none in VR.

**2. Get oriented**

- **What you do:** Push the graph sideways with an open palm to spin it a half turn; grab it to stop it; grab it with both hands and pull apart to make it about twice as large; carry it a little closer.
- **What you see:** The graph turns with momentum and slows under friction. You count 16 families and 20 marriage ties; Pucci floats alone, attached to nothing.
- **Controls:** push, grab (catch and carry), stretch.

**3. Find the Medici**

- **What you do:** Grab the blank text magnet from the rack; its text field opens. Say "Medici" (or type it on the floating keyboard), and set the magnet down just outside the graph. Then poke the Medici node.
- **What you see:** The Medici node slides out of the graph toward the magnet, trailing a short streak, and stops at the magnet; its marriage ties stretch behind it. Poking it opens its readout: name, 6 marriages, its attributes, and that the text magnet is moving it. You lift the magnet back to the rack and the Medici slide home.
- **Controls:** grab, speak, release (place), poke, lift out.

**4. Who they married into**

- **What you do:** Stretch an empty bin's mouth to about a hand's width and leave it beside the graph. Grab the Medici node and draw it slowly out of the graph, then carry it over the bin and release.
- **What you see:** While you hold it, the edges behave as springs that weaken with each hop: the six families married to the Medici (Acciaiuoli, Albizzi, Barbadori, Ridolfi, Salviati, Tornabuoni) hang close beneath it, families two marriages away barely lean, the rest stay put. Released over the bin, the Medici and every family hanging within the bin's mouth drop through it. Each node springs back to its place in the graph wearing a ring in the bin's color and leaves a labeled bead in the bin; the bin reads 7. Had a second-hop family slipped in, you would grab its bead and throw it out.
- **Controls:** stretch (the bin's mouth), grab, carry, release into a bin.

**5. Who matters most (PageRank)**

- **What you do:** Grab the centrality well from the rack. Its label reads PageRank (poke the label to pick another centrality member). Set it down just below the graph.
- **What you see:** As the well nears the graph its field lines reach up into the graph and a tick marks the moment it comes into reach. On release, PageRank runs and every node is pulled toward the funnel in proportion to its score, with short trails: the Medici travel furthest, Pucci barely moves. A PageRank magnet and a PageRank lamp appear on the rack.
- **Controls:** grab, poke (optional), release (place).

**6. Read the result**

- **What you do:** Grab the well and twist it clockwise to strengthen its pull, release it slowly where it is, then poke it.
- **What you see:** A stronger pull spreads the families down the funnel in score order, highest deepest. The readout lists the ranking: Medici about 0.144, Guadagni about 0.097, Strozzi about 0.087, Albizzi about 0.078, Tornabuoni about 0.071, down to Pucci about 0.010. Poking any node now also shows its PageRank and which laws are moving it.
- **Controls:** grab, twist, release, poke.

**7. Color and size by PageRank**

- **What you do:** Grab the new PageRank lamp from the rack and set it above the graph; carry it higher until its cone covers every family; twist it to widen the size range; release.
- **What you see:** The cone's edge is visible as field lines. Nodes inside it take a color ramp and swell by PageRank; the Medici are largest and warmest. Lowering the lamp narrows the cone to fewer families; raising it again covers all. Once released, the lamp stays where it is, so your arm can come down.
- **Controls:** grab, carry (reach), twist (strength), release (place).

**8. Keep only strong families (filter)**

- **What you do:** Grab the well's rim and slide it up until the rim reads 0.070 (it steps in detents of 0.005; poke the rim to type or say an exact value), then press the hourglass cap.
- **What you see:** As the rim rises, families whose score clears it drop into the funnel; the others drift outward and dim. The rim shows "0.070 -- 5 kept" (Medici, Guadagni, Strozzi, Albizzi, Tornabuoni) and names the nearest family below it (Ridolfi, 0.069), so you see who just missed. The press flashes the scene once, time stops, the eleven dimmed families are hidden, and the hourglass gains a second line of sand. The arrangement, the lamp's style and the filter are now committed.
- **Controls:** grab the rim and slide, press the hourglass cap (freeze).

**9. Write a note**

- **What you do:** Pull a note slip off the pad; its text field opens. Say "Medici married across factions -- compare with Strozzi" (or type it), and drop the slip onto the Medici node.
- **What you see:** The slip sticks to the Medici and follows the node wherever it moves. Poking it reopens the text.
- **Controls:** grab, speak, drop onto.

**10. Undo a mistake**

- **What you do:** Reaching for the note pad, you knocked the rim up to 0.09 and pressed freeze; only the Medici and Guadagni remain. Turn the hourglass over, slide the rim back to 0.070, and press the cap again.
- **What you see:** Nothing had changed since that freeze, so the flip unfreezes it: time runs again, Strozzi, Albizzi and Tornabuoni reappear dimmed just outside the rim, and the note is still on the Medici (it was written before the mistaken freeze). Lowering the rim drops them back in. The new freeze replaces the mistaken one, whose pale line disappears from the history strip. For a mistake since the last freeze (the wrong lamp, the wrong magnet), lift the object out and the nodes relax back, or flip once to return to the last freeze.
- **Controls:** flip the hourglass (undo), grab the rim and slide, press the cap (freeze); lift out for one unfrozen object.

**11. Save**

- **What you do:** Press the lid of the specimen jar. The first time, a name field opens: say "Florentine marriages, PageRank" or type it.
- **What you see:** If anything has changed since the last freeze, the jar freezes first (one flash). The jar shows the project name and the number of freezes it holds. The saved project keeps the history, every field object as its layout, run, filter or style layer, the bin as a group, the note, and the simulation's seed.
- **Controls:** poke (the lid), speak.

### Advanced journeys

- **Compound rule (PageRank above 0.07 AND wealth above 50, OR in the Medici bin):** hang a second well under the first well's drain and drop the wealth magnet into its drain; only families that fall through both are kept (AND). A well set beside the first keeps what falls into either (OR). Flip a well to make it a hill that pushes matches out (NOT). Drop the Medici bin into the first well's funnel and its members count as kept regardless. The structure of the rule is visible as the stacking of the wells, and poking the bottom well's readout spells the rule out as text.
- **Tuning algorithm options:** poke the well and choose Options; each option grows a peg on the rim (damping, iterations, weight attribute). Twisting a peg changes the value, the algorithm reruns at each detent, and you watch the families slide as damping moves from 0.85 to 0.5. That makes sensitivity visible: a family that barely moves is robust to the option.
- **Comparing two runs:** set a PageRank well on one side of the graph and a betweenness well on the other. Each node settles between them in proportion to its two scores, a tug of war in the spirit of Dust and Magnet: families strong on both hang in the middle close to the line between the wells, families strong on one lean to its side. Freeze, then lift one well out and in again to replay the difference. For the same algorithm with two settings, set two PageRank wells with different damping pegs.
- **Layers on subsets:** drop the PageRank lamp onto the Medici bin and only the bin's members are styled. Or stand a fence through the graph and hang a lamp on one side: the fence stops its cone. Each lamp is its own style layer; lamps stack in the order you hung them, and the lamp's readout moves it up or down the order. Lifting one out removes its layer.
- **Recipes:** throw the field objects of a finished, frozen arrangement (the well with its rim setting, the lamp) into an empty bin. A bin of field objects is a recipe: it goes on the rack and stays there across projects, and dropping it onto any graph places the same objects in the same relative positions and runs them.
- **Joining tables:** stays on the 2D page. A join has no physical meaning and no useful motion; forcing it into a field object would hide what it does.

### How it scales

- **60+ algorithm catalog:** the rack holds one well per family (about eight); the drawer lists every algorithm grouped by family with a search field (dictation or keyboard), and an algorithm pulled from the drawer is a well with that member already set. Algorithms whose result is not a per-node score get a different shape of the same idea: communities become several small basins that pull each community together, a shortest path becomes a cord stretched between two nodes that the path's nodes slide onto, connected components drift apart.
- **New object types:** anything with a numeric value per node becomes a magnet and a lamp; per-edge values set the edges' spring stiffness. An object type with no quantity (a note, a project, a saved view) gets a passive object that sticks or holds but exerts no force. Every object, passive or not, gets a readout listing its verbs.
- **Plugins:** a plugin algorithm that declares a numeric per-node output gets a well in its family's drawer section automatically, with option pegs generated from its declared options. A plugin layout is a magnet-like law if it can run as forces; a one-shot layout is placed as a "settle" weight that pulls nodes toward its computed positions, so it still blends with other laws.
- **The limit:** motion stops being readable well before the simulation stops being computable. Trails and individual travel are useful up to roughly a few hundred visible nodes; beyond that the simulation runs on the GPU and the display shows density flow rather than nodes. More than three or four live laws at once produce arrangements nobody can explain; anything that is configuration rather than a quantity (data import, joins, schema edits, export settings) stays on the 2D page.

### On each device

| Device            | Hands                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Controllers / eyes                                                                                                                       | Honest notes on WebXR                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| ----------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Meta Quest 3 / 3S | Full vocabulary through WebXR Hand Input (25 joints per hand); far grab and far poke use the hand's target ray, the same ray the system shows. Throw speed estimated from recent joint positions.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Touch controllers: grip grabs, trigger pokes, stick flick pushes, X freezes, Y undoes. Detent ticks through the gamepad haptic actuator. | The best target: both inputs and controller haptics. Pose velocity for throws where the browser reports it, otherwise estimated. No eye tracking on Quest 3/3S. Do not count on the Web Speech API in Quest Browser; plan on the in-page keyboard. Two hands touching each other track poorly, which is why nothing here needs one hand on the other.                                                    |
| Samsung Galaxy XR | Full vocabulary through Chrome's WebXR hand tracking, with target rays for far grab and poke.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Galaxy XR controllers (sold separately) exposed as WebXR gamepads; ticks where the haptic actuator is exposed.                           | Eye tracking drives the system UI but is not given to web pages as continuous gaze, so nothing here depends on it. Chrome's Web Speech API is the likeliest dictation path; test it inside an immersive session. Hands-only users get audible clicks instead of rumble.                                                                                                                                  |
| Apple Vision Pro  | Look and pinch: a quick pinch pokes, a held pinch grabs (Safari's transient-pointer input gives a ray toward what you looked at when the pinch began, then follows the hand). Direct touch poke, push, twist and flip use hand-tracking joints, which a page must request and the user permit. Without that permission the page still works on look and pinch alone: twist is read from the pinching hand's orientation, and push is replaced by throwing the graph. | PS VR2 Sense controllers on visionOS 26 appear to WebXR as tracked controllers; check whether their haptics reach the page.              | No haptics for hands: detent clicks are audible, and field strength shows as the density of field lines. Gaze is only available at the moment of a pinch, so there is no hover highlight; the grabbed object outlines the instant you pinch, and a wrong grab released slowly changes nothing. Check whether Safari's speech recognition runs during an immersive session; the keyboard is the fallback. |

### Why it should work

- **Dust and Magnet (Yi, Melton, Stasko and Jacko, 2005)** showed that attribute magnets pulling data points by value are learnable by people with no visualization training, and that moving magnets is a natural way to compare attributes.
- **Kinetica (Rzeszotarski and Kittur, CHI 2014)** used physics on a tablet (magnets, sifters, barriers) for multivariate data and reported that people explored more of the data than with a spreadsheet.
- **BumpTop (Agarawala and Balakrishnan, CHI 2006)** showed tossing and piling with physics as a usable way to organize documents; throwing into a bin is the same act.
- **VR physics games** (Half-Life 2's gravity gun, Half-Life: Alyx's gravity gloves, Job Simulator, Boneworks) made grab, throw and catch the most practiced interactions in VR; players need no tutorial for them, and gravity gloves are the model for the far grab here.
- **Force-directed layout is already a simulation** that people watch settle; the layout package in this repository ships steppable ForceAtlas2 and Fruchterman-Reingold simulations, and webgpu-graph-algorithms runs ForceAtlas2 on the GPU, so adding forces is an extension of what already runs, not a new engine.
- **Motion is a strong perceptual signal:** the eye picks out the one node that moves among still ones faster than a color difference, so watching a result arrive should make it easier to notice than recoloring alone.
- **An explicit freeze** gives the live simulation a clear unit of history, where an always-live mix has no natural point to undo to; Superhot VR shows that people readily grasp time as something they control.

### What it would teach us

- Does watching nodes move under a law explain a result better than seeing a color appear? Do people remember who moved?
- Is the explicit freeze welcome (a feeling of control, a clean undo) or a chore people forget, leaving long stretches uncommitted?
- Do people understand the two kinds of undo step (discard changes since the last freeze; unfreeze the last freeze), or do they expect every flip to do the same thing?
- How accurate is throwing into a bin with hands versus controllers, and how much catch assistance does it need before it stops feeling physical?
- Does a rim on a funnel make thresholds easier to choose, because you see who is just below it?
- How many laws can someone stack before they can no longer predict or explain the arrangement?
- Does an inertial graph help orientation (a sense of weight and place) or cause discomfort compared with a graph that moves only while held?
- Is twist-for-strength discovered without being told, and is flip-for-reverse understood the same way on a magnet, a well, a bin and the hourglass?
- Do positions produced by forces mislead people about the data (reading closeness as similarity when it is an artifact of several laws at once)?

### Known risks

- **Superposition is hard to read:** with several laws, a node's position has several causes. Mitigation: poking a node lists the laws acting on it and their share; each object's field lines have their own hue and a label; a soft cap warns past four live laws.
- **Position is only an approximate encoding:** forces settle at equilibria that bend values. Mitigation: exact numbers are always one poke away (node readout, well ranking); freeze captures the exact state so it does not keep shifting while you read.
- **Non-determinism:** the same objects could settle differently on another run. Mitigation: the simulation takes a seed and the project stores it; freezes store positions, not just laws.
- **Throws miss:** Mitigation: bins have a catch radius that grows with throw speed, a near miss slides in, and carrying and releasing over the bin always works.
- **Releases that throw by accident:** a hand-tracked pinch release jitters. Mitigation: a release counts as a throw only above about 1 m/s; anything slower places.
- **Grabbing the wrong thing in a dense graph:** a node and the graph share space. Mitigation: the target highlights before the pinch closes (outlines on pinch on Vision Pro), a slow release of the wrong grab changes nothing, and stretching the graph spreads its nodes apart.
- **Motion sickness from a drifting graph:** Mitigation: only the graph moves, never the viewer; strong friction and a cap on spin speed; the tether returns it softly; pushing can be switched off for a held-only graph; a reduce-motion setting replaces travel with a short fade to the new positions (this gives up the motion feedback, so readouts carry more of the load).
- **Arm fatigue:** holding a lamp above the graph or twisting at chest height tires the shoulder. Mitigation: every object stays where it is released; far grab moves objects without reaching; twist ratchets by regrabbing; the rack sits at elbow height; the graph can be carried closer and lower.
- **Seated and one-handed use:** Mitigation: the rack sits in front at elbow height, not beside the hip; far grab and far poke reach everything; every two-handed verb is also a button in the object's readout.
- **Accidental pushes and twists while reaching:** Mitigation: a push needs an open flat palm moving faster than a threshold and is ignored while anything is held; twist clicks in detents and only applies while an object with a dial is held.
- **Undo for unfrozen work is coarse:** between freezes, one flip discards every change since the last freeze, not just the latest one. Mitigation: lifting out a single object undoes just that law; the discarded changes stay as a pale line in the history strip, so the flip itself can be redone. Untested risk: people may need to freeze more often than they want to get fine-grained undo.
- **No haptics on hands:** Mitigation: audible detent clicks and the field-line density stand in for rumble.
- **Dictation is uneven across browsers:** Mitigation: voice only fills text fields, and the floating keyboard always works.
- **Large graphs:** live motion of thousands of nodes is unreadable and costly. Mitigation: the GPU simulation, trails only on the nodes inside a law's reach, and density display past a few hundred visible nodes.
- **Not everything is a force:** algorithms with structural results and configuration tasks fit badly. Mitigation: basins, cords and drift for the common structural results; everything else stays on the 2D page rather than being forced into a metaphor.

### Comparison row

| #   | Name        | One-line idea                                                                                                                                                 | Mode model                           | Navigation                             | Selection                                              | Command surface                                                           | Parameters                                                                             | Undo                                                                     |
| --- | ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| 15  | Physics Lab | The graph is a live simulation: set magnets, wells, lamps, bins and fences into it, watch nodes move under them, and press the hourglass to freeze and commit | applied laws, explicit freeze commit | inertial push, grab to catch and carry | throw or carry into bins, fall into wells past the rim | elbow-height rack of field objects + catalog drawer + per-object readouts | twist a dial, distance for reach, rim height for threshold, exact entry in the readout | lift the object out; turn the hourglass over (Y); history strip for redo |

## Prototype 16: Proofreader's Slate

A stylus on a slate that shows the graph flat and unoccluded; you edit it with proofreader's marks, and the ink is a script you can run again.

### How it differs, layer by layer

| Layer                   | Proofreader's Slate                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Differs from                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Mode model              | Marks on a surface, not commands in the air. Each proofreader's mark is a verb on whatever ink or object it touches. Where a stroke starts decides what it means: on the frame it moves the view, on the plate it is a mark or a note, in the margin it is a command or a number. The ink stays and is the session's script.                                                                                 | Spellcasting (9) draws glyphs in the air and they vanish after the cast; Gesture Grammar (3) flicks are transient; Point and Say (2) uses voice. Here there is no voice and no air stroke, and every command stays on the page as a readable record.                  |
| Navigation              | The slate shows a flat, de-cluttered copy of the graph. Strokes that start on the slate's frame move the view: slide along the left ruler to zoom, along the bottom ruler to turn, pull from either ruler into the plate to pan. With the slate resting, the off hand may also pinch, twist and drag on the plate. The big graph in front of you follows the slate's framing. You never touch the big graph. | Workbench (5) steers with a 3D world in miniature; Voodoo Dolls (14) pulls 3D copies of regions; Palette Hand (1), Gesture Grammar (3) and Spellcasting (9) grab the graph itself.                                                                                    |
| Selection               | Ink on the flat view: a circle selects, an underline takes a node with its neighbors, a line between two nodes names a pair, a bracket in the margin takes a span of a ranked list.                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Lasso in 3D in Gesture Grammar (3) and Context Menus (11); ring and sweep in Spellcasting (9); in and out points on a strip in Cutting Room (23). Nothing here is occluded, because the plate is flat.                                                                |
| Menus / command surface | No menu. About ten fixed marks cover the common verbs. Every other verb is a word written in the margin, completed from the catalog's plain names, and only from the verbs valid for the object the word is led from. A leader line from ink to the margin sets the word's scope.                                                                                                                            | Workbench (5) types into a command line; Hand of Cards (13) plays verb cards; Context Menus (11) opens menus at the object.                                                                                                                                           |
| Parameters              | Handwritten numbers in the margin: strike one value and write the new one beside it. Tapping a value offers nearby values to tap, for anyone who would rather not write. A cut line drawn across a histogram is fine-tuned by sliding the nib along the cut line, away from the histogram: the farther away, the finer the movement.                                                                         | Spoken numbers in Point and Say (2), typing in Workbench (5), finger counts in Hand Signs (7), trigger pressure as strength in Tool Belt (4). Here distance along the line sets precision, not amount, and it works the same with a pen, a controller or a fingertip. |
| Feedback                | Each recognized mark is redrawn neatly in place, with its reading in the margin ("hide: 6 nodes"). Ink the slate could not read stays gray until you correct it. Hovering the nib over anything on the slate lights the same thing in the big graph; hovering over plate ink also scrolls the margin to that ink's reading.                                                                                  | Point and Say's (2) sentence chips show a pending command; here the reading confirms a step already taken, written where you wrote.                                                                                                                                   |
| Undo                    | Strike through a step's reading in the margin to remove the step; later steps replay without it, and any step that needed it turns gray and says what it is missing. Write "stet" beside a struck reading to restore it.                                                                                                                                                                                     | Cutting Room (23) also edits history, but on a timeline of clips with a playhead. Here history is the written page itself, struck and restored like a manuscript, with no timeline and no scrubbing.                                                                  |

**Setup:** solid colored background in full VR; the graph floats at chest height 70-80 cm away. The slate is about 40 by 30 cm, landscape, like a large drawing tablet, so margin text stays legible on current headsets. It lies on a real desk, or on a rigid board (a clipboard, a tray, a large book) on the lap, which gives real touch under the nib. Standing, or with no surface, it floats fixed in place at waist height, tilted toward you like a lectern; it can also ride in the off hand like a clipboard, at the cost of arm fatigue and no real touch. The nib is a tracked stylus (Logitech MX Ink on Quest), a controller held like a pen, or the dominant index fingertip. No voice. No air strokes. Pen buttons and pressure are deliberately unused, so the vocabulary is the same on every device and every task works with one hand.

### The control vocabulary

One meaning per input, everywhere. The dominant hand's nib inks, unless the stroke starts on the frame, where it moves the view. The off hand never inks.

| Input                                                               | Meaning                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Circle (closed loop) around objects on the plate                    | Select them. A circle around nodes selects those nodes; around an edge's midpoint tick, that edge; around a galley row, a histogram bar, a note or a reading, that object.                                                                                                                                           |
| Underline below a node's label (plate only)                         | Select that node and its neighbors. Double underline: two hops.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Line from one object to another                                     | Name the pair (for verbs that take two things, such as a path between two nodes or a comparison of two runs).                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Delete loop (a single stroke through objects, ending in a pigtail)  | Hide them: adds a filter step. Nothing is destroyed.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Caret (^)                                                           | Insert here. In the margin between two readings: the next word becomes a step at that point in the script. On empty plate: add a node.                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Swap mark (the S-shaped transpose mark), spanning two reading lines | Reorder those two steps (for style layers, which one paints on top). It must cross both lines, so the letter S in a word never reads as a swap.                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Leader line from ink out to empty margin                            | The word written at its end acts on that ink. A margin word with no leader acts on the whole graph.                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Word written in the margin                                          | A command: a verb from the catalog's plain names ("pagerank", "color", "size", "path", "find", "save"). After two letters, up to five completions appear beside it; only verbs valid for the object at the start of the leader are offered. A word written on a bracket or on a line in the margin acts on that ink. |
| Word written on the plate                                           | A note, kept as your handwriting, attached to the nearest object or to the one its leader touches. A word on the plate is never a command.                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Tap (a dot) on something offered                                    | Pick it: a completion, a candidate reading for gray ink, a nearby value, "more" at the end of a list, "apply" under a proposed step. A dot anywhere else does nothing.                                                                                                                                               |
| Number written in the margin                                        | Set the value of the reading on the same line.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Strike through one value in a reading                               | Replace that value: it waits, gray, until you write or tap the new one beside it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Strike through a reading's verb or the whole reading line           | Remove that step; the graph replays without it. The ink stays, struck, and the plate ink it came from is shown struck too.                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| "stet" written beside a struck reading or value                     | Restore it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Cut line drawn across a histogram                                   | A threshold. Keep the nib down and slide it along the cut line, away from the histogram: the farther out, the slower the cut moves (half speed, quarter speed, a tenth). Slide back to move it 1:1.                                                                                                                  |
| Bracket in the margin beside rows of a list                         | Select that span (on a ranked list, those nodes; on readings, those steps).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Stroke that starts on the left ruler                                | Slide along it: zoom. Pull it into the plate: pan (you drag the paper).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Stroke that starts on the bottom ruler                              | Slide along it: turn the big graph (the plate re-flattens from the new direction). Pull it into the plate: pan.                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Stroke that starts on the margin's outer edge                       | Scroll the margin.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Off hand on the plate (slate resting)                               | One finger drags to pan; two fingers pinch to zoom and twist to turn. Same as the rulers; never ink.                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Hover (nib near the slate, not touching)                            | Light the hovered object in the big graph and show its name and values in the margin; over plate ink, scroll the margin to its reading. Does nothing else.                                                                                                                                                           |

### The slate

| Region                                                                                      | What it holds                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Plate (left two thirds)                                                                     | The flat view: the current layout seen from the big graph's viewing direction, with overlapping nodes fanned apart so every node and label is visible, and a small tick at each edge's midpoint. Marks and notes go here. |
| Margin (right third; mirrored to the left for left-handed users)                            | One reading per step, numbered top to bottom: the script. Galleys (ranked lists with a small histogram) appear under the reading of a run. Command words and numbers go here.                                             |
| Frame: rulers (left and bottom edges; mirrored with the margin) and the margin's outer edge | Zoom, turn and pan; scroll the margin. Strokes that start here never ink.                                                                                                                                                 |
| Title line (top edge)                                                                       | The page's name, written by hand; it names the saved project and any recipe drawn from it. A leader from the title line gives the project's verbs (save, rename, export).                                                 |
| Back of the slate                                                                           | A printed card of the marks. Turn a held slate over to read it. On a desk or lectern, write "?" in the margin: the galley that opens lists the marks as well as the catalog.                                              |

Every object has its verbs through the same path: circle it or start a leader on it, and the margin word completes only from that object's verbs. Nodes, edges and selections live on the plate; runs and results are galleys; filter steps, style layers and notes are readings; the project is the title line.

| Mark             | Proofreading meaning       | graphty verb                      |
| ---------------- | -------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
| Circle           | (circled = an instruction) | select                            |
| Underline        | emphasis                   | select with neighbors             |
| Line between two | (leader)                   | pair                              |
| Delete loop      | delete                     | hide (filter step)                |
| Caret            | insert                     | insert a step or a node           |
| Swap mark        | transpose                  | reorder steps or layers           |
| Strike through   | (cross out)                | remove a step, or replace a value |
| stet             | let it stand               | restore a step or a value         |
| Bracket          | (set as a block)           | take a span                       |

### The journey

1. **Enter**
    - **What you do:** Put on the headset and pick up the pen. At a desk, touch the nib to three points on it once; the slate lies down there. On the lap, do the same on a board. Standing, the slate appears as a lectern at waist height.
    - **What you see:** The Florentine families graph floating in front of you; the same 15 families drawn flat on the slate's plate; an empty margin with the page title "Untitled".
    - **Controls:** None beyond picking up the pen; the three-tap calibration if there is a surface.

2. **Get oriented**
    - **What you do:** Start a stroke on the left ruler and pull it into the plate to pan. Slide along the bottom ruler to turn the big graph a quarter turn. Hover the nib over a few nodes.
    - **What you see:** The big graph pans and turns to match the plate's framing; the plate re-flattens after the turn. Each hovered family lights in the big graph, and its name, degree and wealth appear in the margin.
    - **Controls:** Pull from a ruler (pan), slide along the bottom ruler (turn), hover.

3. **Find the Medici**
    - **What you do:** Circle the Medici node on the plate.
    - **What you see:** The circle is redrawn as a neat ring; the margin reads "1 select: Medici (1 node)"; the Medici light in the big graph.
    - **Controls:** Circle. (In a large graph: write "find" in the margin, then "Medici"; completion offers node labels after "find", and the found node is circled for you.)

4. **Who they married into**
    - **What you do:** Underline the Medici label.
    - **What you see:** Six families join the selection on the plate and in the big graph; the margin reads "2 + neighbors: 6 (Acciaiuoli, Albizzi, Barbadori, Ridolfi, Salviati, Tornabuoni)".
    - **Controls:** Underline.

5. **Who matters most (PageRank)**
    - **What you do:** Write "pa" in the margin with no leader line, so it acts on the whole graph. Tap "pagerank" among the completions.
    - **What you see:** The reading "3 PageRank: whole graph, damping 0.85" and, under it, a galley: the 15 families ranked with their scores and a small histogram.
    - **Controls:** Margin word, tap a completion.

6. **Read the result**
    - **What you do:** Hover down the galley; pull along the margin's outer edge to scroll if it is long.
    - **What you see:** Medici first at 0.146, then Guadagni 0.098 and Strozzi 0.088. Each hovered row lights its family in the big graph and on the plate.
    - **Controls:** Hover, margin-edge scroll.

7. **Color and size by PageRank**
    - **What you do:** Draw a leader line from the galley's PageRank column header out into empty margin and write "color" (completion offers only what a result column can do). Draw a second leader from the same header and write "size".
    - **What you see:** "4 color by PageRank" and "5 size by PageRank"; the big graph and the plate recolor and resize, Medici largest.
    - **Controls:** Leader line, margin word.

8. **Keep only strong families (filter)**
    - **What you do:** Draw a vertical cut line across the galley's histogram near the low end. It reads 0.060, which would keep Salviati (0.061). Keep the nib down and slide it up along the cut line, away from the histogram; the cut now creeps at a quarter speed, and you ease it to 0.065. Lift, then draw a delete loop through the bars left of the cut. (Or strike "0.060" in the cut's label and write "0.065", or tap it and pick 0.065 from the nearby values.)
    - **What you see:** The reading "6 hide: PageRank below 0.065 (6 nodes)". Salviati, Barbadori, Pazzi, Ginori, Lamberteschi and Acciaiuoli fade out of both views; nine families remain.
    - **Controls:** Cut line, slide along it for fine movement, delete loop (or strike and write a value, or tap a nearby value).

9. **Write a note**
    - **What you do:** Write "marriage hub; bank" in open space on the plate and draw a short leader from it to the Medici.
    - **What you see:** Your handwriting is set neatly beside the Medici on the plate and pinned to the Medici in the big graph; the margin reads "7 note on Medici".
    - **Controls:** Words on the plate, leader line.

10. **Undo a mistake**
    - **What you do:** Trying a thought, you loop out Strozzi, and the margin reads "8 hide: Strozzi (1 node)". You want Strozzi back. Strike through that reading. (Struck the wrong one? Write "stet" beside it and it comes back.)
    - **What you see:** The reading and the loop on the plate are both shown struck, Strozzi returns in both views, and every later step replays without it.
    - **Controls:** Strike through a reading; "stet" to restore.

11. **Save**
    - **What you do:** Write "Florentine power" on the title line, then write "save" in the margin.
    - **What you see:** "saved: Florentine power, 8 steps, 1 struck". The page, its ink and the graph state are the project. If a project by that name already exists, the reading stays gray with "replace saved Florentine power?" and an "apply" to tap.
    - **Controls:** Title line, margin word, tap to confirm a replace.

### Advanced journeys

- **Compound rule:** write "wealth" in the margin to get its histogram, then cut it and loop out the low side. Two hide steps in a row are AND. For OR, bracket the two readings in the margin and write "or" on the bracket; the reading becomes "hide: PageRank below 0.065 or wealth below 40".
- **Tuning algorithm options:** the PageRank reading lists its options in the margin ("damping 0.85, iterations 100"). Strike 0.85 and write 0.7 beside it; the step reruns and everything after it replays. To set an option that is not shown, write its name and a number on the reading's line; completion offers only that algorithm's option names.
- **Comparing two runs:** with the caret, insert a second "pagerank" step with damping 0.7. Draw a line from one galley's header to the other's, then write "compare" on that line (it lies in the margin, so the word is a command). A third galley lists each family's two ranks and the change, largest change first.
- **Path between two nodes:** draw a line from one node to another on the plate, then a leader from that line out to the margin, and write "path". A word written on the plate itself would be a note.
- **Layers on subsets:** circle a group, lead it to the margin and write "color", then write the color name or a number on the same line. The layer applies only to that circle. Swap marks between two style readings reorder which paints on top.
- **Recipes:** bracket a run of readings (say, PageRank, color, size, cut) and write a name on the bracket. On any other project, write that name in the margin, with a leader for a scope or none for the whole graph. The steps arrive as gray, proposed readings and the big graph previews their result; tap "apply" under them to run them. Each step then stands as its own reading, which you can strike, renumber or retune like any other. Steps that picked nodes by hand stay out of a recipe; a "find" step with a rule travels.
- **Joining tables:** stays on the 2D page of the graphty app. Matching columns by handwriting is slower and riskier than a table on a screen. Once saved as a recipe, the join can be run from the slate by its name.

### How it scales

- **60+ algorithm catalog:** there is no menu to outgrow. Any verb is a word, and completion after two letters narrows it to five candidates. Writing "?" in the margin opens the catalog as a galley; how it is grouped and ordered is the app's choice, and tapping a row writes the word for you, so a whole session can be done with circles, leaders and taps.
- **New object types:** anything that shows up on the plate or in the margin can be circled and led to a margin word. Completion offers only the verbs valid for the object at the start of the leader, so new types add words, not marks.
- **Plugins:** a plugin's algorithms, layouts and formats arrive with their plain names and options, so they can be written and completed at once. Plugins add words, never marks: the set of about ten marks stays fixed.
- **The limit:** the plate is flat. Graphs of thousands of nodes need the plate to show clusters that you circle and zoom into. Tasks that are truly about depth (3D layout, spatial structure) belong to the big graph, which this scheme only lets you watch. Writing is slower than pointing for one-off picks, so a session of many small unrelated commands favors a pointing scheme.

### On each device

| Device                                                               | Nib                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Surface and touch                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Notes on what WebXR exposes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Meta Quest 3 / 3S (hands and controllers)                            | Logitech MX Ink: tracked position and orientation, a tip sensor that reports real contact, a haptic pulse on each recognized mark. Without it, a Touch controller held like a pen, or the index fingertip.                                     | Desk, board on the lap, or lectern. With MX Ink, every real contact re-anchors the slate's plane, so a board that shifts on the lap stays in step. Logitech's MX Mat if present.                                            | Quest Browser exposes MX Ink to WebXR as a tracked input source; the tip value is used only to detect contact. Confirm its input profile on hardware before building. Hand tracking gives fingertip joints for the fingertip nib and the off-hand pinch. Using the off hand's fingers while holding MX Ink needs hands and stylus tracked at once; confirm that in Quest Browser. Nothing in the scheme depends on it: the rulers do the same with one hand. |
| Samsung Galaxy XR (hands and controllers)                            | No known stylus for WebXR. A controller held like a pen, or the index fingertip.                                                                                                                                                               | Desk, board or lectern after calibration. With the fingertip, the real surface is the only touch there is, so it matters more here. Contact is inferred from the calibrated plane, with a visual and sound tick at contact. | Chrome on Android XR exposes hand joints and controllers to WebXR. Raw eye gaze is not exposed, and this scheme does not need it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Apple Vision Pro (eyes and hands; PS VR2 controllers where relevant) | Index fingertip as the nib, from hand tracking. PlayStation VR2 Sense controllers, supported from visionOS 26, held like a pen where Safari exposes them. Logitech Muse is a stylus for visionOS apps; it is not known to be exposed to WebXR. | Desk, board or lectern after calibration with the fingertip. No haptics on the fingertip: a visual and sound tick at contact.                                                                                               | Safari exposes hand joints (with the hand-tracking feature) and the transient pointer. The scheme reads only fingertip joints, so the same vocabulary works. Confirm controller support in Safari WebXR on the target visionOS version.                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| All devices                                                          |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | No browser on these headsets offers a handwriting recognizer to the page, so the page carries its own: a small shape recognizer for the ten marks, a digit recognizer for numbers, and word matching against a closed list (catalog names, option names, node labels, recipe names) narrowed by the first two letters. Notes are never recognized; they stay as ink.                                                                                         |

### Why it should work

- **Real touch makes strokes accurate.** Lindeman, Sibert and Hahn (1999) found that a physical surface under virtual widgets made manipulation faster and more accurate. Arora et al. (CHI 2017) measured drawing in VR and found strokes much more accurate against a physical surface than in mid-air. The slate is built to sit on one.
- **Pen and pad in VR is old and sound.** The Personal Interaction Panel (Szalavari and Gervautz, 1997) and the Virtual Notepad (Poupyrev et al., 1998) put a tracked pen and pad in VR for commands and handwriting.
- **Editing marks have shipped and people use them.** Microsoft 365's Ink Editor (strike through to delete, caret to insert, circle to select) and Apple's Scribble on iPad (scratch out to delete, circle to select, a vertical line to split or join) teach almost this exact mark set.
- **Pen acts, the other hand moves.** Hinckley et al., "Pen + Touch = New Tools" (UIST 2010), showed that people naturally write with the pen and manipulate with the other hand. Here the off hand only moves the view, and the frame lets the pen hand move it too when the off hand is busy or absent.
- **Sliding away for finer control is familiar.** Media scrubbing on iOS slowed the scrubber to half, quarter and fine speed as the finger slid away from the bar; the cut line works the same way.
- **Ink as a command that stays.** InkSeine (Hinckley et al., 2007) kept search ink as part of the notes. Scriboli (Hinckley et al., 2005) used a drawn delimiter to separate scope from command, as the leader line does here. Sketchpad (Sutherland, 1963) put the pen on the model itself.
- **A flat view removes occlusion.** Picking in a dense 3D node-link drawing fights overlap and depth. On the plate every node has its own spot, and the big graph keeps the 3D view for looking.
- **A shipped stylus exists for the target headset.** Logitech MX Ink works with Quest 3 and 3S and is used by design apps on that headset, so a pen is not a lab prop.
- **The record is readable.** A marked-up page shows what was done, in order, to whoever reads it next, the way a proof does for an editor.

### What it would teach us

- Does working on a flat, unoccluded view while watching a 3D mirror beat acting on the 3D graph directly for analysis tasks, in time and in errors?
- How much does the real surface matter? Test the same slate on a desk, on a board on the lap, and as a lectern with nothing under it.
- Is handwriting with completion fast enough for verbs compared with picking from a menu, and after how many letters do people stop writing and tap?
- Do people go back and edit earlier steps on the page (strike, retune, reorder), or only add at the bottom?
- Can about ten marks plus words carry a whole session, and what share of commands are marks rather than words?
- How often do people look up at the big graph, and does working head-down on the slate cost them the 3D structure?
- Is sliding along the cut line for finer movement learnable, or do people strike and write the number instead?
- Do frame-start strokes for the view ever get mistaken for ink, or ink for a view move?
- Will analysts reuse their pages as recipes, and can someone else read a page they did not write?

### Known risks

- **Recognition errors.** Mitigation: a small mark set chosen to be distinct in shape and in place (an underline is below a label and only on the plate, a strike is through a reading in the margin, a swap mark must span two lines); gray ink with up to three candidate readings to tap; marks redrawn neatly so a misreading is visible at once.
- **Marks read out of handwriting** (a looped letter in a note crossing a node and reading as a delete loop). Mitigation: a stroke that starts within a short time and distance of the previous stroke is read as part of the same word, never as a mark; a delete loop must be one stroke with a pigtail at its end; any hide is a filter step, undone by one strike.
- **Neck and arm fatigue from working head-down, or from holding the slate up.** Mitigation: rest the slate on a desk or a board, or use the lectern; let the slate tilt toward the face; at a desk the slate and the big graph both fit in one view, so a glance up is an eye movement more than a head movement; holding the slate in the hand is optional, never required.
- **Drift between the virtual slate and the real surface,** so the nib "touches" a little early or late. Mitigation: MX Ink's tip sensor reports true contact and re-anchors the plane on every touch; a hover band with a tick at contact; recalibrate with three taps.
- **No pen on Galaxy XR and Vision Pro.** Mitigation: a fingertip or a controller as the nib, a larger margin writing area, completion after two letters, taps on nearby values instead of written numbers, and a vocabulary that never needed pressure or pen buttons.
- **Handwriting is hard for some people** (tremor, limited grip, an injured writing hand). Mitigation: every verb can be reached by "?" and a tap, every number by tapping nearby values, and the whole scheme works with one hand; the slate mirrors for either hand.
- **Text too small to read on a headset.** Mitigation: a 40 by 30 cm slate, readings that wrap, and text never smaller than about 0.6 degrees of view; zooming the plate never shrinks the margin.
- **Two views to reconcile.** People may lose track of which plate node is which in the big graph, especially where the plate fans overlapping nodes apart. Mitigation: hover lights the same object in both; the big graph always follows the plate's framing and turn.
- **People who do not know proofreader's marks.** Mitigation: the mark card on the back of the slate and in the "?" galley; most marks match Word's and iPad's ink editing; anything can be written as a word instead.
- **Dense graphs on a flat plate.** Mitigation: clusters drawn as single marks until zoomed; overlapping nodes fanned apart; zoom with the ruler.
- **Removing an early step breaks later ones** (striking PageRank leaves color and size with nothing to read). Mitigation: those readings turn gray and say what they are missing; "stet" brings the struck step back and they run again.

### Comparison row

| #   | Name                | One-line idea                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Mode model                                                                    | Navigation                                                                                      | Selection                                                              | Command surface                                                           | Parameters                                                                 | Undo                                      |
| --- | ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| 16  | Proofreader's Slate | A stylus on a slate (on a desk, a board on the lap, or a lectern) shows the graph flat and unoccluded; proofreader's marks edit it, margin words name the other verbs, and the ink is a script you can run again | marks on a surface; where a stroke starts sets its meaning; ink is the script | strokes from the rulers (zoom, turn, pan), optional off-hand pinch and twist; big graph follows | circle, underline (node + neighbors), line between two, margin bracket | fixed marks + handwritten margin words with completion filtered by object | handwritten or tapped numbers; cut line, slide along it for finer movement | strike through a reading; "stet" restores |

## Prototype 17: Orbits

Eyes and head only: commands circle whatever you look at, each with its own moving dot; following a dot picks it, a nod confirms and a head shake backs out.

### How it differs, layer by layer

| Layer                   | Orbits                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Closest existing prototypes and how this differs                                                                                                                                                                     |
| ----------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Mode model              | Look, follow, nod. Looking at something (fixation) only sets the focus and never changes anything. Only smooth pursuit -- tracking a dot that moves on a circle, a movement nobody makes by accident -- picks a verb. Verbs that change the project become pending and show their effect as a preview; a nod commits, a shake backs out.                                | HUD (10) also aims with the head and eyes, but fires with a pinch. Point and Say (2) and Context Menus (11) are select-then-command with hands. Here no hand ever confirms.                                          |
| Navigation              | "Turn" is a verb on the graph: pick it, and while it is active, turning your head turns the graph around its own center three times as far; nod to keep the new angle, shake to spring back. Follow the "go" dot on a node to fly to it; "Frame all" flies back. Leaning in is ordinary parallax and never a command.                                                   | Two-hand grab (1, 3), turntable stick (4), miniature (5), palms (9), look-and-pull plus radar (10). No other prototype moves the view with the head alone.                                                           |
| Selection               | Fixation sets one focus. Where nodes overlap in depth, each candidate's outline carries a dot running a different circle, and following one picks it. The selection is built by following "+ add" (this node) or "+ ring" (its neighbors); selection acts at once, with no nod, because it changes nothing in the project.                                              | Point and pinch (1), lasso (3, 11), painting (4), typed query (5), tongs (6), ring and sweep (9), lock-on (10). None picks by following a moving mark.                                                               |
| Menus / command surface | Rings of up to 8 static, labeled tiles around the focus, each tile with a small dot orbiting it on its own direction and phase. The dots move only while your gaze is inside the ring. Following "more" opens the next ring; the algorithm catalog is three rings deep.                                                                                                 | Marking menus (3) are flicked by hand; runes in Spellcasting (9) orbit but are picked by hand; HUD (10) strips are fixed at the edge of the view. Here the menu is around the object and picked by the eyes or head. |
| Parameter entry         | A dial: a static scale with two dots on its rim, one running clockwise, one counterclockwise. Follow the clockwise dot to raise the value, the other to lower it; the value moves fast, then slower the longer you follow. A quick head tilt steps one notch; a held tilt keeps stepping, faster the further you tilt. Voice numbers are optional.                      | Sliders (1), wrist twist (3), stick dial (4), scrubbing (5), knobs (6, 8), finger counts (7), pull apart (9), gauges (10), in-menu steppers (11). All of those take a hand.                                          |
| Feedback                | A followed dot's tile fills as the pursuit match builds. A pending tile pulses and the graph previews its effect (fading what would be hidden or removed, showing what would be added). A short rising tone confirms a nod, a short falling tone a shake, and the word "Kept" or "Cancelled" shows beside the focus. A live count ("5 of 15 kept") rides on every dial. | Sound here confirms only the two head gestures; it is not a sonification of data.                                                                                                                                    |

**Setup:** Fixed environment for every prototype: solid colored background, full VR, graph floating at chest height 70-80 cm away. The user is seated or standing with hands at rest. Inputs: eye gaze where the device gives it to the app, head pose on every device, optional voice. Hands and controllers are not used; stray pinches and button presses are ignored unless the accessibility switch (below) is on.
