**The queue strip.** A row of chips floating 3 cm above the thumb-side edge of the forearm, next order at the wrist end. While append is on, each press adds a chip and nothing runs. Turning append off runs them in order; the running chip fills as it works, and a one-line status under the graph repeats it so you can watch the graph instead. After a queue finishes, its chips stay grey on the strip until the next order, so you can drag the whole run onto the object column to keep it as a recipe. An order undone with Cancel last leaves a hollow chip at the end of the strip until the next new order; dragging it back onto the strip redoes it.

**Seated and one-handed.** The card follows the forearm wherever it rests, so a seated person can lay it on a thigh or an armrest. In Tune on the graph, "card placement: in the air" pins the card and the group bar side by side, 1.5 times larger, low and to the left of the graph, pressed by fingertip or by ray click; append and subtract are then the latches. The card is pinned in the air automatically when only one hand or controller is tracked.

### The journey

1. **Enter.**
    - **What you do:** From the 2D page, enter VR. The first time, touch your off wrist crease and then your off elbow crease with the right fingertip (or controller tip) so the grid fits your forearm. Turn your off palm up.
    - **What you see:** The Florentine families at chest height. The command card lights on your forearm with the graph's verbs; dark cells are the ones that need a selection.
    - **Controls:** Two calibration touches, once per person; palm up.

2. **Get oriented.**
    - **What you do:** Grab the graph and turn it. Press Label (C4) to turn all names on, and Inspect (A1) for a summary. Turn your palm down and hold camera view 1 to keep this overview.
    - **What you see:** Family names on the nodes; a readout beside the graph: 15 families, 20 marriages, and their attributes. Camera cell 1 shows a stored mark.
    - **Controls:** Grab (fist / grip); touch C4, A1; palm down, hold camera 1 (A3 of the bar) for 0.6 s.

3. **Find the Medici.**
    - **What you do:** Spot the name near the middle and click it. With the palm still down, hold selection group 1 to store it.
    - **What you see:** The Medici node selected; turning the palm up shows node verbs (Grow and Link to light up). Group 1 on the bar shows "1".
    - **Controls:** Click (pinch / trigger); hold group 1 for 0.6 s (controllers: off stick flicked up and held).

4. **Who they married into.**
    - **What you do:** Palm up. Hold the append modifier, press Grow (C1), then Frame (B1), and let go. Store the result as group 2.
    - **What you see:** Two chips appear on the queue strip, Grow then Frame. On release they run in order: the six families married to the Medici join the selection with their marriage edges, then the view frames the grown selection. Group 2 shows "7".
    - **Controls:** Off fist (off grip) held; touch C1, B1; release; palm down, hold group 2 (off stick flicked right and held).

5. **Who matters most (PageRank).**
    - **What you do:** Click empty air to clear the selection. Palm up, press Analyze (A3); the sub-grid opens on page 1, centrality. Press PageRank at its wrist corner (A1).
    - **What you see:** A PageRank chip on the queue strip fills and clears; a "PageRank 1" tile appears in the object column. The card returns to the graph's verbs.
    - **Controls:** Click empty air; touch A3, then A1. Voice alternative: "PageRank".

6. **Read the result.**
    - **What you do:** Click the "PageRank 1" tile. Press Inspect (A1).
    - **What you see:** The card shows run verbs. A ranked readout opens beside the graph, Medici first, then Guadagni and Strozzi, with values; pointing at a row lights that family in the graph, and clicking a row selects it.
    - **Controls:** Click the tile; touch A1.

7. **Color and size by PageRank.**
    - **What you do:** With the run still selected, hold the append modifier, press Color by (A4) and Size by (B4), and let go.
    - **What you see:** Two chips, then the graph recolors and resizes by PageRank in that order. Both orders act on the run, which stays selected; two layer tiles appear in the object column.
    - **Controls:** Off fist (off grip) held; touch A4, B4; release.

8. **Keep only strong families (filter).**
    - **What you do:** With the run selected, press Keep only (C2). Touch the value cell (B3) and slide toward the elbow to raise the threshold, or tap minus (A3) and plus (C3); then tap the value once to accept.
    - **What you see:** All cells but row 3 and Cancel last dim. Families below the value fade as a live preview, with "kept: n of 15" beside the value. On accept, the rest are hidden and a filter step tile appears.
    - **Controls:** Touch C2; slide from B3 along the arm (controllers: slide the controller tip); tap A3 / C3 for single steps; tap B3.

9. **Write a note.**
    - **What you do:** Palm down and tap group 1 to recall the Medici. Palm up, press Note (A5). Speak the note, or spell it on the keypad. Press A5 again to finish.
    - **What you see:** The card becomes a phone keypad; the text grows on a note card next to the Medici. On finish, the note pins to the Medici and a note tile appears in the column.
    - **Controls:** Tap group 1 (off stick flicked up); touch A5; dictation or keypad; touch A5.

10. **Undo a mistake.**
    - **What you do:** With the Medici selected, you queue Label (C4) and Frame (B1), but your finger lands on Remove (B2) between them. Before letting go, pinch the Remove chip on the queue strip and pull it away. Let go. Then you decide the labels clutter the view. Hover over Cancel last (C5): the status line says "Cancel: Frame". Press it twice.
    - **What you see:** Three chips, the middle one Remove; pulled away, it vanishes and the other two close up. On release, labels show and the view frames the Medici. The first Cancel last undoes Frame and the view returns; the second undoes Label. Two hollow chips sit at the end of the strip; dragging the Frame chip back would redo it.
    - **Controls:** Off fist held; touch C4, B2, B1; pinch-drag the chip away (trigger-drag); release; hover, then touch C5 twice.

11. **Save.**
    - **What you do:** Press Save (B5). It is lit on the command card for every object type.
    - **What you see:** A "Saved: Florentine families, Oct 9" line under the graph; a double tick on controllers. To rename later, select the graph, press Tune (C3), press the name option and use the keypad.
    - **Controls:** Touch B5.

### Advanced journeys

- **Compound rule.** Control groups are the set algebra. "Strong families married into the Medici, plus the Strozzi": recall group 2, hold append and tap group 3 (the Strozzi, stored earlier) to take the union, let go, then press Keep only (C2). Select the PageRank run and press Keep only again with a threshold. Filter steps in the column apply in order, so two steps are AND; append is OR; subtract is AND NOT. Inspect on a filter step names what it removes.
- **Tuning algorithm options.** Click the run tile, press Tune (C3): the option list shows damping, iterations and weight attribute, one per cell. Press damping, step it from 0.85 to 0.7 on the stepper, accept; the tile now reads "options changed". Press Analyze (A3) to run it as "PageRank 2". PageRank 1 keeps its results and the options that made them.
- **Comparing two runs.** Select "PageRank 1", press Compare (B3): the pointer becomes a targeting cursor. Click the "PageRank 2" tile. A comparison run appears whose values are the rank changes; Inspect reads it, Color by paints it, Keep only shows only families that moved more than a step size.
- **Layers on subsets.** Select a style layer, press Link to (A2): targeting cursor. Turn the palm down and tap group 2 (a group cell is a valid target), and the layer now paints only those seven families. Painting a subset directly works too: recall group 2, press Color by (A4), and step through the palette on the stepper.
- **Recipes.** A queue is a recipe in waiting. After a queue runs (for example Grow, Grow, Analyze PageRank on these, Frame: rank the two-hop neighborhood and look at it), its grey chips stay on the strip; drag them onto the object column to keep them as a recipe tile. Later, select the recipe tile and press Link to (A2), then click a target node, group or the graph: the orders replay there, as a queue you can still pull chips from before it runs. Inspect lists its steps; Tune exposes the numbers inside it.
- **Joining tables.** Stays on the 2D page. Choosing join keys and columns is a form, and a 15-cell arm grid is the wrong place for it. The joined columns appear in VR as attributes that Color by, Size by and Keep only can use.

### How it scales

- **60+ algorithm catalog.** Twelve per page in fixed categories gives six pages; every algorithm has a permanent address such as "Analyze, page 2, B3", the same for every user and every session, so practice transfers the way grid hotkeys do. A page indicator runs along the side of the sub-grid. Saying a name (optional) presses the cell and briefly shows its address, which teaches it. Nothing is ever reordered by recency, because that would undo what the arm has learned.
- **New object types.** A new type maps its actions onto the 15 existing verbs and leaves the rest dark. Actions that fit no verb go behind Tune as option cells. A type may not move a verb or claim a cell for a new verb.
- **Plugins.** A plugin's algorithms take new pages after the built-in ones, in the order the plugins were installed; built-in addresses never change. A plugin's object types follow the rule above.
- **The limit.** Fifteen primary verbs, and one level behind Analyze and Tune. The grid cannot grow: a fingertip on a forearm, under current hand tracking, cannot reliably hit cells smaller than about 3 by 4 cm, so 3 by 5 is near the ceiling (and on a short forearm, the pinned card in the air is the way to keep cells that size). Past about ten algorithm pages, paging gets slow and a search on the 2D page (or voice) has to carry the long tail.

### On each device

| Device                                     | Inputs used                                                                                    | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Meta Quest 3 / 3S, controllers             | Rays, triggers, grips, thumbsticks, controller tip pressed on the forearm, haptics             | The primary build. Meta Quest Browser exposes controller poses, buttons and haptic pulses to WebXR. The off hand holds its controller with the palm turned up, which keeps the inner forearm in view; the right controller's tip (a fixed offset from its grip pose, set by the calibration touches) is the stylus, and touching it to the arm is real touch. No eye tracking reaches WebXR.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Meta Quest, hands                          | Pinch and fist on the right hand; fist and palm turn on the off hand; fingertip on the forearm | WebXR Hand Input is supported in Meta Quest Browser. Palm-toward-you plus pinch is Quest's system menu gesture, so the off hand never pinches, and the app ignores select events from the off hand. The forearm is not tracked; the grid is placed from the wrist pose and the calibration touches. A hand touching the other forearm partly hides it from the cameras, so contact is detected with a tolerance and the hover light carries the aim.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Samsung Galaxy XR, hands and controllers   | Same as Quest                                                                                  | Chrome on Android XR supports WebXR with hand input and with the optional controllers. Android XR also reserves a palm-facing pinch for system navigation, so the same off-hand rule applies. The headset tracks eyes for its own interface, but as far as we know gaze is not exposed to web pages, so this prototype does not use it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Apple Vision Pro, eyes and hands           | Look-then-pinch (transient pointer), hand joints for the forearm grid, fists and palm turn     | Safari's WebXR gives a transient pointer that aims where you look at the moment of the pinch, then follows the hand; that drives click, double-click and the box drag. Hand-tracking joints are available in immersive sessions, which is what the arm grid needs. Any pinch, by either hand, makes a transient pointer; the app keeps only the dominant hand's. Looking at an upturned palm shows the Home indicator, which is why the card starts 4 cm up the forearm; whether a held off fist ever counts as the Home tap has to be checked on device, and if it does, append on Vision Pro is the latch. No haptics: the touch on the arm is the only confirmation besides the lit cell and a short sound. No continuous gaze, so there is no hover on the graph before a pinch; hover on the arm still works, because it comes from the fingertip joint. |
| Apple Vision Pro, PS VR2 Sense controllers | Same as Quest controllers                                                                      | visionOS 26 added PS VR2 Sense controller support, including for WebXR in Safari per Apple's release notes; check button and haptic coverage on device before relying on it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Voice, all devices                         | Web Speech recognition                                                                         | Only where the browser offers speech recognition inside an immersive session; check per device. Nothing in the journey needs it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |

### Why it should work

- Strategy games have tested this control set for decades with players who act hundreds of times a minute. Box select, numbered control groups, camera hotkeys, shift-queued orders and a fixed command grid (StarCraft II's "grid" hotkey layout) exist because a fixed spatial layout plus stored sets lets experts act without looking.
- Spatially stable command layouts beat changing ones for experienced users: CommandMaps (Scarr, Cockburn, Gutwin and Bunt, CHI 2012) were faster than ribbons and menus once people knew them, because position is remembered.
- Touching a real surface helps in VR. Lindeman, Sibert and Hahn (CHI 1999) found that a hand-held physical panel under virtual buttons made presses faster and more accurate than floating panels. Mine, Brooks and Sequin (SIGGRAPH 1997) showed body-relative controls use proprioception so they can be found without looking.
- The body itself works as that surface. Skinput (Harrison, Tan and Morris, CHI 2010) put buttons on the forearm; Gustafson, Rabe and Baudisch (CHI 2013) found the tactile cue of touching your own palm mattered more than seeing it for palm-based interfaces.
- Shipped precedent for off-hand surfaces: MRTK's hand menus on HoloLens 2. (The palm-up gestures of Quest and visionOS are precedent too, and also the reason this design keeps the off hand from pinching.)
- Analysis has natural sequences (select, grow, run, color, filter), and a queue lets you state the whole sequence once and watch it run, with a chance to fix it before it does.

### What it would teach us

- Whether hand tracking is precise enough for a fingertip on your own forearm, or whether this only works with a controller tip as the stylus.
- Whether people learn cell positions by feel within one session, and how many presses it takes before they stop looking at the arm. The corner cells have landmarks (wrist and elbow creases); the middle ones may not.
- Whether queuing is used in analysis, where most orders finish at once, or whether it only pays for slow algorithms and recipes.
- Whether numbered selection groups map to how analysts think about subsets (the family, its in-laws, the top ten), and whether four are enough.
- Whether stored camera views matter in a graph that fits in front of you, and whether "tap to see, tap twice to move" prevents surprise motion.
- Whether a 3D box is a useful selection shape in a node-link layout, or whether people want a lasso.
- Whether hover previews on the arm are seen at all, since the eyes are on the arm, not the graph, at the moment of hovering.
- How long people hold the off forearm up before resting it, seated and standing, and how often they switch to the card in the air.

### Known risks

- **Arm fatigue from holding the forearm up, palm up (supination is tiring to hold), and from reaching across the body.** Mitigation: the grid follows the forearm wherever it rests, so a seated user can lay it on the thigh or armrest; the graph does not move when the arm does; the card can be pinned in the air; voice spares the arm for common actions.
- **Hand tracking loses a hand that touches the other forearm.** Mitigation: the press commits on lift, using the last good fingertip position; cells are about 3 by 4 cm and the hover light shows the aim before contact; controllers, whose tip is tracked by the controller, are the primary build.
- **The forearm is not tracked, so the grid can drift off the real arm.** Mitigation: per-person calibration of wrist and elbow, a grid anchored at the wrist and laid along the measured direction, and a quick recalibration by touching the elbow crease again.
- **System gestures on the off hand.** A pinch with the palm toward you opens the system menu on Quest and Android XR and the Home view on visionOS. Mitigation: the off hand never pinches (no thumb-to-fingertip shortcuts, no pinch modifier); off-hand select events are ignored; the card starts up the forearm, away from the palm. Open item: on Vision Pro, confirm the off fist never registers as the Home tap.
- **Accidental presses when the arm brushes the body or the other hand.** Mitigation: only the side facing you takes presses, only the dominant index tip or controller tip counts, and a press needs contact then lift without a slide.
- **Adjacent-cell slips (Remove next to Keep only).** Mitigation: hover previews show what a cell would do before contact; destructive verbs sit away from the wrist corner where the eyes first land; every order is undone by Cancel last; queued orders can be pulled off before they run.
- **Hands lose the subtract modifier as a pose.** Subtract is a latch on the group bar on hands, which costs a palm flip. Mitigation: subtract is rarer than append, and group algebra (where it is used most) already happens on the group bar.
- **Queue confusion for people who never played strategy games.** Mitigation: without append, every order runs at once, like a button; the queue only exists while append is on.
- **Many-meaning Cancel last.** Mitigation: it always acts on the newest order, whatever its state, and hovering over it names what it is about to cancel.
- **Temporary cards reuse cells (B3 is Compare on the card, the value on the stepper).** Mitigation: every cell a temporary card does not use goes dark, and the elbow corner always backs out.
- **Motion when views change.** Mitigation: you never move; recalling a camera view or sliding with the stick moves the graph against a solid background, at a steady speed, with no acceleration, after a preview outline.
- **No haptics on Vision Pro with hands.** Mitigation: the touch on the arm is real; the lit cell and a short sound confirm the press.
- **Clicking empty air by mistake clears the selection.** Not solved here: the stored groups are the recovery. If testing shows it matters, the previous selection becomes a fifth, automatic group.

### Comparison row

| #   | Name         | One-line idea                                                                                                                                          | Mode model                                                                                       | Navigation                                                                                                         | Selection                                                                                                                     | Command surface                                                                                         | Parameters                                                        | Undo                                                                                       |
| --- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| 22  | Command Card | Real-time-strategy controls: 3D box select, control groups for selections and camera views, a fixed 3x5 command grid on the off forearm, queued orders | select, then orders from a fixed grid; append (held or latched) queues orders to run in sequence | camera control groups (tap to see, tap twice to move the graph), double-tap a group to frame it, stick slide, grab | 3D box between two corners; double-click for all of a kind; four selection groups on the back of the forearm or the off stick | 3x5 forearm grid with a fixed verb per cell and hover previews; paged algorithm sub-grid behind Analyze | targeting cursor; stepper on the grid with a forearm-length scrub | pull an order off the queue strip; fixed Cancel last cell; drag a hollow chip back to redo |

## Prototype 23: Cutting Room

A film editor's timeline under the graph holds every change as a clip on a track; you edit the history, not the state, and compare runs by branching.

### How it differs, layer by layer

| Layer                   | Cutting Room                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | Differs from                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| ----------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Mode model              | Edit the history, not the state. Every action lands as a clip at the playhead; the graph is whatever the enabled clips left of the playhead produce. Changing, moving or disabling an old clip replays everything after it. A clip is the object: pressing it loads it into the source monitor with its knobs and its own verbs. | Select-then-command (Palette Hand, Point and Say, Gesture Grammar, Context Menus) and tools (Tool Belt, Workshop, Grip and Tool) all act on the present. Workbench edits properties of the present. Spellcasting's wind-time rewinds and throws away what came after; here the future survives an edit to the past. |
| Navigation              | Free grab moves the graph and is never recorded. Mark a view to keyframe the graph's pose on the camera track; forward playback at normal speed moves the graph between keyframes. A shuttle (off stick, or off-hand pinch and slide) moves the playhead back and forward.                                                       | Two-hand grab (Palette Hand, Gesture Grammar), Workbench's miniature, HUD's radar, Tool Belt's auto-frame. None records views or plays them back as a sequence.                                                                                                                                                     |
| Selection               | Mark In and Out on a ranked strip of nodes (a range by rank), or on the ruler (everything that changed in that span of steps). Pointing at a node or edge also works; consecutive picks gather into one selection clip.                                                                                                          | Point and pinch (Palette Hand), lasso (Gesture Grammar, Context Menus), tongs (Workshop), lock-on (HUD), typed query (Workbench). None selects by rank range or by "what changed between step 4 and step 9".                                                                                                        |
| Menus / command surface | Six tracks, three one-shot edit tools (razor, ripple delete, slip), a searchable clip drawer that opens on the verbs of the object at the playhead, and a source monitor that previews one clip with its verbs.                                                                                                                  | Hand-held palette (Palette Hand), marking menus (Gesture Grammar), belt and rack (Tool Belt), docks (Workbench, Context Menus), HUD strips. Here the command surface and the history are the same object.                                                                                                           |
| Parameters              | Trim a clip's edge to change its main number (a filter's threshold): a scale pops out under the edge while you hold it, and moving the hand away from the scale makes it finer. Knobs on the clip in the source monitor for the rest; press any value to type it. Every change replays downstream live.                          | Sliders (Palette Hand), scrubbing (Workbench), wrist twist (Gesture Grammar), finger counts (Hand Signs). All of those change the present; a trim here changes a past step and its consequences.                                                                                                                    |
| Feedback                | The graph shows the state at the playhead. Before an edit lands, a ripple bar counts the clips it will replay; later clips flash in order as they replay. After a fork, a diff lane lists what was added, removed or changed between the two branches and marks those elements on the graph.                                     | Results feed (HUD), history lists (Workbench, Context Menus), receipts on a rail (Workshop). None shows a difference between two lines of work.                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Undo                    | Two levels. Undo (off Y, or the Undo chip) takes back the last edit to the timeline, whatever it was: a clip landing, a trim, a knob turn, a delete, a fork. Older mistakes are fixed in place: change, disable or ripple-delete the old clip and keep every later step. Fork to compare instead of undoing.                     | Undo buttons, words and gestures (Palette Hand, Point and Say, Gesture Grammar, Tool Belt, Workbench, Hand Signs) unwind a stack, losing later work to reach an old mistake. Workshop's receipt pull only removes the latest step.                                                                                  |

**Setup:** solid colored background in full VR; the graph floats at chest height 70-80 cm away. The timeline is a gently curved band about 70 cm wide, 45-50 cm in front of you and about 40-45 cm below eye level (placed from your head height on entry, so it works seated or standing), tilted about 35 deg toward your eyes, inside arm's reach so it can be touched directly, with the hands resting low. The source monitor and the clip drawer stand at its two ends, angled about 30 deg in toward you. A handle under the timeline moves it (lift it to the graph's lower edge, or bring it closer). Dominant hand is chosen on the 2D page and can be swapped from the project chip. Devices: Meta Quest 3 / 3S and Samsung Galaxy XR with controllers or hands; Apple Vision Pro with eyes-and-pinch and hands, PS VR2 Sense controllers where Safari exposes them. Voice is optional and only fills text (drawer search, notes).

### The control vocabulary

One meaning per input, everywhere. Every off-hand input also has a chip on the timeline, so the whole prototype works with one hand.

| Input (controllers / hands)                                  | Meaning                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Dominant trigger / dominant pinch                            | Take hold of what the ray or fingertip is on: press a button, pick a node or edge, move a clip, trim a clip edge, turn a knob, drag the playhead, drag an In-Out bracket. On empty space it takes hold of the world. A press acts on release; moving more than 2 cm first makes it a drag (a node row or a column header dragged out becomes a chip you can drop). |
| Grip, either hand / closed fist, either hand                 | Grab the world: move and turn the graph. A second hand joining a held grab (grip, fist, or off-hand pinch) scales. Never recorded until you mark the view.                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Off stick / off-hand pinch, hold and slide sideways          | Shuttle the playhead. Stick: tap left or right steps one clip; hold deflected to shuttle, faster the farther you push; let go to stop. Hands: pinch, slide left or right, speed grows with distance from where you pinched; release stops; a short nudge and release steps one clip. Transport chips (step back, play, step forward) on the ruler do the same.     |
| Off stick click / "End" chip on the ruler                    | Jump the playhead back to the end, where new clips append.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Off X / bracket gutter or In and Out chips                   | Mark on the strip or ruler under the dominant ray: first press In, second press Out; the strip says which comes next ("next: Out"). On hands, pinch-drag along the strip's or ruler's gutter from one row or step to another.                                                                                                                                      |
| Off Y / Undo chip at the timeline's left end                 | Undo the last timeline edit. Hold Y or long-press the chip to open the edit list and step back several.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Off trigger                                                  | Nothing, on purpose: a resting squeeze never acts.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Dominant A / "Mark view" chip on the camera track head       | Keyframe the graph's current pose as a clip on the camera track.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Dominant B / drawer handle                                   | Open the clip drawer at the playhead, on the verbs of the object there (a selection's verbs when a selection is in effect, the graph's verbs when none is).                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Dominant stick flick / tool chips at the timeline's left end | Load an edit tool for one use: up razor, down ripple delete, left or right slip. It returns to the pointer after one action; stick click cancels. The loaded tool is named at the ray tip or fingertip, and hovering a clip with it previews its effect (a struck-through clip and the ripple bar for ripple delete).                                              |
| Eye button on any clip or track head                         | Enable or disable that clip or the whole track.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Drop a chip or clip onto a clip                              | Labeled drop zones appear on the target, only the ones valid for that pair: "Add" (a node to a selection clip, a column to a style or filter clip), "Replace" (same kind of clip; everything after replays), "Any of" (a filter onto a filter, forms an OR group), "Paint only these" (a style onto a selection, links them).                                      |
| Haptic tick / audio tick                                     | The playhead or a trim edge crossed a clip boundary or a value step.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

### Key structures

**The tracks** (top to bottom; a clip's horizontal position is its order, not wall time). A track with no clips is folded to a thin labeled bar that still accepts drops and opens when its first clip lands.

| Track                            | Holds                                                                    | Example clips                    |
| -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------- |
| Camera                           | Keyframed graph poses                                                    | "Start", "Medici close"          |
| Notes                            | Notes, attached to the selection in effect at that step, or to the graph | "Central by marriage"            |
| Style                            | Style layers and layouts                                                 | "Map PageRank to color and size" |
| Filter                           | Filter steps; side by side is AND, an "any of" group is OR               | "Keep PageRank above 0.07"       |
| Analysis                         | Algorithm runs and their results                                         | "PageRank"                       |
| Selection                        | Picks of nodes or edges, ranges, expansions                              | "Medici", "+ Neighbors, 1 hop"   |
| Diff lane (appears after a fork) | What differs between the two branches                                    | "4 nodes changed rank"           |

At the head of the timeline sits a locked data clip ("Florentine families"; pressing it shows the graph's verbs) and the project chip (Save, Exit, Branches, swap hands).

**The edit tools** (each is one-shot)

| Tool              | On a clip                                                                                         | On the ruler                                         |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Pointer (default) | Move, trim, open in the source monitor                                                            | Drag the playhead; press "Branch here" to fork       |
| Razor             | Split a compound clip (a recipe, an "any of" group) into its steps                                | Fork: split the timeline into two branches from here |
| Ripple delete     | Remove the clip; later clips close the gap and replay                                             | Remove a marked In-Out span                          |
| Slip              | Shift a clip's window and keep its width (ranks 1-5 become ranks 2-6; a color ramp's range moves) | --                                                   |

Two style bands that split one range share an edge: trimming that edge moves the cut for both.

**The source monitor** shows one clip alone with its knobs and a verb row for that object (Disable, Duplicate, Rename, Branch from here, Save as recipe, Delete). With a clip that produces a node value loaded (a run, a column), it becomes the **ranked strip**: the nodes in order of that value, one row each, with a bracket gutter for In and Out. With nothing loaded it lists nodes by name. Marking Out lands a selection clip such as "Ranks 1-5 by PageRank", which stays a rule: if PageRank changes upstream, it still holds the top five.

**A marked span on the ruler** is an object too: its bar offers "Select what changed", "Save as recipe" and "Disable span".

**The clip drawer** has a search field at the top, a recent row, the verbs of the object at the playhead, then sections by track (Selection, Analysis, Filter, Style, Notes) and, inside Analysis, by category and by the kind of value an algorithm returns. Each algorithm row shows its cost estimate. Choosing a clip inserts it at the playhead; dragging it places it anywhere. Search uses an in-world keyboard, or dictation where the browser offers speech recognition.

### The journey

**1. Enter**

- **What you do:** press Enter VR on the 2D page.
- **What you see:** the Florentine families graph at chest height; below it the timeline with the locked data clip at its head, one "Start" keyframe on the camera track, the other tracks folded to thin bars, and the playhead parked at the end, where new clips land.
- **Controls:** none. Touching either stick, or the "?" chip at the timeline's left end, shows a three-line legend for the shuttle, Mark and Undo.

**2. Get oriented**

- **What you do:** grip and turn the graph, add the second grip to scale it up; press A to keep this view.
- **What you see:** the graph moves with your hand; a "View 1" keyframe clip appears on the camera track.
- **Controls:** grip or fist (grab the world); A or the "Mark view" chip.

**3. Find the Medici**

- **What you do:** in the source monitor's node list (by name, since nothing is loaded), point at "Medici" and press the trigger. Or point at the Medici node itself.
- **What you see:** hovering a row (or bringing a fingertip within 3 cm of it) lights its node and draws a leader line to it. Pressing lands a "Medici" clip on the Selection track and the node is selected on the graph.
- **Controls:** dominant trigger / pinch.

**4. Who they married into**

- **What you do:** press B; the drawer opens on the selection's verbs. Choose "Neighbors". (Or type or say "neigh" in its search.)
- **What you see:** a "+ Neighbors, 1 hop" clip lands after "Medici"; the selection grows to the Medici and their six marriage partners (Acciaiuoli, Albizzi, Barbadori, Ridolfi, Salviati, Tornabuoni).
- **Controls:** B or the drawer handle; trigger / pinch on the drawer row.

**5. Who matters most (PageRank)**

- **What you do:** in the drawer, open Analysis, Centrality (or search "pagerank") and choose PageRank.
- **What you see:** a PageRank clip lands on the Analysis track and fills left to right as it runs, then shows a small distribution of its values on its face. The graph does not change yet: a run is a fact, not a style.
- **Controls:** B; trigger / pinch.

**6. Read the result**

- **What you do:** press the PageRank clip.
- **What you see:** the source monitor turns into the ranked strip by PageRank: Medici on top, then Guadagni and Strozzi, with each value. Hovering a row lights the node. Knobs for damping, iterations and weight sit above the strip.
- **Controls:** trigger / pinch on the clip; hover to read.

**7. Color and size by PageRank**

- **What you do:** drag the PageRank header from the source monitor onto the Style track's bar.
- **What you see:** the Style track opens with a "Map PageRank" style clip; the graph colors and sizes every node by its value. Its knobs (color ramp, size range) appear in the source monitor; turning one recolors live.
- **Controls:** trigger / pinch drag; drop on a track.

**8. Keep only strong families (filter)**

- **What you do:** drag the PageRank header onto the Filter track's bar, then take the new filter clip's right edge and pull it.
- **What you see:** while you hold the edge, a PageRank scale pops out under it with the count kept; the graph hides weaker families as you pull, with a tick at each value step. Raising the hand above the scale makes each centimeter count for less. Release at about 0.07; the clip returns to its normal width and prints "above 0.07". Pressing that value lets you type it instead.
- **Controls:** trigger / pinch drag on the clip edge (the trim).

**9. Write a note**

- **What you do:** with the playhead at the end, press B and choose "Note"; type on the in-world keyboard or dictate "Central by marriage, not by wealth".
- **What you see:** a note clip on the Notes track, attached to the selection in effect there (the Medici and their partners), a pin on that group in the graph, and the text on the clip face.
- **Controls:** B; trigger / pinch; in-world keyboard or its microphone key.

**10. Undo a mistake**

- **What you do:** first, a fresh mistake: you just dropped a stray "Hide labels" clip. Press Y (or the Undo chip). Second, an older one: the selection clip says "+ Neighbors, 2 hops" because you chose the wrong row from the drawer's recent list, so the note covers too many families. Press that clip and turn its hops knob back to 1.
- **What you see:** the stray clip leaves and the labels come back; nothing else replays. While you turn the hops knob, the ripple bar reads "replays 5 clips", and every clip after the Neighbors clip flashes in order as it replays; the selection shrinks, the note re-attaches to the smaller group, PageRank, style and filter stay as they were. If the result is wrong, Y puts the knob back.
- **Controls:** Y or the Undo chip; trigger / pinch on the clip and its knob. To look back first: shuttle back on the off stick (or off-hand pinch and slide), stick click to return to the end.

**11. Save**

- **What you do:** press Save on the project chip.
- **What you see:** a "Saved" marker on the ruler (a marker, not a step). The whole timeline is saved: every clip, disabled ones, branches and camera keyframes. Playing forward from the start later replays the session as a walk-through, with the graph easing between keyframed poses.
- **Controls:** trigger / pinch on the project chip.

### Advanced journeys

- **Compound rule:** two filter clips side by side on the Filter track are AND ("PageRank above 0.07", then "degree at least 3"). For OR, drag the second onto the first and drop on its "Any of" zone; they nest into a group clip marked "any of". The razor splits the group back into its steps.
- **Tuning algorithm options:** open the run in the source monitor and turn damping or iterations; style and filter clips downstream replay as you turn. For an algorithm whose estimate is over about half a second, the knob shows a ghost value and replays on release, with the estimate printed on the clip.
- **Comparing two runs:** with the playhead just before the PageRank clip, press "Branch here" on the ruler (or razor the ruler there). Only the tracks that will differ are doubled; the rest stay shared. In the upper branch, turn damping to 0.5, or drop a Betweenness clip on PageRank's "Replace" zone. The diff lane appears between the branches; dragging the playhead into it marks the graph by added (plus badge), removed (ghost outline) and changed (delta mark), each also colored, and the ranked strip shows each node's rank in both branches side by side. New clips land on the branch the playhead is on; the other is dimmed. Pressing a branch's head shows that branch alone.
- **Layers on subsets:** make the subset first as a selection clip (or a rank range from the ranked strip), then drop a style clip on its "Paint only these" zone; a chain mark shows the link, and the style paints only that set. Moving or editing the selection clip carries the style with it.
- **Recipes:** mark In and Out on the ruler around a span of clips and press "Save as recipe" on the span's bar. It becomes a compound clip in the drawer; dropping it into another project inserts its steps and asks to rebind any column it cannot find. The razor opens it into steps.
- **Joining tables:** stays on the 2D page. After a join, the data clip at the head of the timeline updates and everything after it replays; clips that no longer apply turn red, are skipped, and say why.

### How it scales

- **60+ algorithm catalog:** the drawer is searched by typing or dictation, browsed by category and by the kind of value returned, and has a recent row. Each clip shows its cost estimate before you drop it. The timeline itself never grows a menu per algorithm: every run is one clip kind on the Analysis track.
- **New object types:** a new object either becomes a clip kind on an existing track (a group is a selection clip, a filter step is a filter clip, a layout is a style clip) or, if it changes state over time and stacks, a new track. Objects map cleanly: graph to the data clip, node and edge to selection clips, run to Analysis, style layer to Style, filter step to Filter, note to Notes, group or selection to Selection, project to the whole timeline and its branches. Each object's verbs live in its source monitor verb row and at the top of the drawer.
- **Plugins:** a registered algorithm, layout or writer appears in the drawer (a writer on the project chip's export list); its knobs are generated from its declared options, and its trim edge maps to its declared main number if it has one. A plugin needs no timeline code.
- **The limit:** long sessions. A few hundred clips need ruler zoom (both hands stretch the ruler) and folded tracks. Replay cost is the hard ceiling: editing an early clip on a 5,000-node graph with slow algorithms downstream is not live, even with cached states every few clips. More than two branches at once is unreadable; others stay folded in the Branches list.

### On each device

| Device                                     | Inputs exposed to WebXR                                                                                                                                                                     | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| ------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Meta Quest 3 / 3S, controllers             | Full xr-standard gamepad: triggers, grips, sticks, A/B/X/Y; haptic pulses                                                                                                                   | The main target. Off hand: stick shuttle, stick click to end, X mark, Y undo, trigger unused, system menu button left alone. Dominant hand: trigger, grip, A view, B drawer, stick flick tools. Haptic ticks make trimming precise. No eye tracking.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Meta Quest 3 / 3S, hands                   | XRHand joints (25 per hand); system pinch as select events per hand                                                                                                                         | Off-hand pinch and slide is the shuttle, read from the select event and the hand's position, so it needs no finger-by-finger detection. Keep the off palm facing down or sideways: a pinch with the palm toward the face is the Meta system menu gesture. Fist from joints. Fingertip proximity gives hover on rows. Audio ticks replace haptics.                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Samsung Galaxy XR, hands and controllers   | Chrome on Android XR: WebXR immersive-vr, hand joints, controllers as gamepads when paired                                                                                                  | Same mapping as Quest; verify button indices and haptic pulses on device. The device tracks eyes for its own system UI; assume gaze is not exposed to pages. Web Speech recognition is available in Chrome for dictation.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Apple Vision Pro, eyes and hands           | Safari WebXR: transient-pointer input (a ray from gaze at the moment of the pinch, then following the hand), one per hand, with handedness; hand joints with permission; no continuous gaze | Look at a clip edge and pinch-drag to trim. Off-hand pinch and slide shuttles (the transient pointer follows the hand; its gaze target is ignored). Dominant pinch on empty space grabs the world; an off-hand pinch joining it scales. Without joint permission there is no hover and no fist: rows light while you pinch and slide along the strip, release on a row picks it, release off the strip cancels. With joints, fingertip proximity gives hover and direct touch works. Dictation: verify Web Speech in immersive Safari; the in-world keyboard is the baseline. |
| Apple Vision Pro, PS VR2 Sense controllers | visionOS 26 pairs them; whether Safari exposes them as WebXR gamepads needs checking on device                                                                                              | If exposed, use the Quest controller mapping; if not, hands only.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

### Why it should work

- Shuttle, step and In/Out marks are the standard of Avid, Premiere, Final Cut and DaVinci Resolve (the J/K/L keys and the shuttle ring on editing consoles); a spring-loaded stick or a pinch-and-slide is the same shuttle ring, and it reads reliably on every headset because it uses only a pinch and a hand position.
- "Edit the history, not the state" ships in parametric CAD: Autodesk Fusion 360's design timeline sits under the model, you roll back to a feature, change it, and every later feature recomputes; SolidWorks has its rollback bar. Houdini and Blender's modifier stack do the same for procedural models.
- Visualization provenance research supports it: VisTrails (Bavoil et al., 2005) keeps a version tree of visualization pipelines and compares versions; Heer et al. (2008), "Graphical Histories for Visualization", found analysts used a browsable history in Tableau to revisit and communicate work; Kurlander and Feiner's editable graphical histories date from 1988.
- VR animation tools (Quill, Tvori) already put a timeline under a 3D scene and edit it by hand in a headset.
- Undo is weak in most XR apps (no Ctrl+Z, invisible stacks). Here the last edit is one press away, and every older step is visible, touchable and fixable in place, so a mistake made five steps ago does not cost five undos or the work done since.
- The timeline sits low and close, so hands work near the lap instead of held up at shoulder height, which keeps arm fatigue down in long sessions.
- A diff answers "what did changing this change?" directly, the way git diff does, instead of asking the reader to compare two pictures from memory.

### What it would teach us

- Do people who have never used an editor understand that changing an old clip changes everything after it, and can they predict the result from the ripple bar before it replays?
- Is "the clip's edge is its value" readable when the scale pops out only while held, or do people still expect clip width to mean time?
- Is pinch-and-slide shuttling reliable and comfortable on Quest and Vision Pro hand tracking, or do people fall back to the transport chips?
- Do people reach for Undo or for fixing the old clip in place, and does having both confuse them?
- How large a graph and how long a history still feel live when an early clip changes?
- Is a diff lane better than two graphs side by side for comparing two runs?
- Do people use the camera track, for example to rehearse or present a walk-through, or only free grab?
- Selecting by rank range on the ranked strip versus pointing at nodes: which do people choose, and for which questions?

### Known risks

- **The concept is heavy for non-editors.** Mitigation: the playhead stays parked at the end, so by default actions simply append and the timeline reads as a history; Undo works the way people expect; editing the past is there when wanted. Empty tracks stay folded until their first clip lands.
- **An edit to the past breaks later clips** (a filter clip whose column a replaced run no longer produces). Mitigation: a broken clip turns red, is skipped, and says why; before an edit lands, the ripple bar counts the clips it will replay and the ones that will break, from the element's plan and estimate; Undo takes the edit back.
- **Inserting in the past surprises people.** Mitigation: when the playhead is not at the end, it reads "insert here" and later clips visibly slide right as a new clip lands; stick click or the "End" chip parks it again.
- **Tool modes cause mistakes.** Mitigation: tools are one-shot, named at the pointer, and preview on hover; ripple delete shows the struck-through clip and the ripple bar before you press.
- **Replay cost.** Mitigation: cached states every few clips, replay only what depends on the change, knobs replay on release for slow algorithms. This needs element work: replay from a step with edited or disabled commands and branches, where today graphty-element's history is a linear undo stack.
- **Looking down at the timeline strains the neck.** Mitigation: the timeline tilts toward you and can be lifted to the graph's lower edge; the graph mirrors the playhead step name so you rarely need to look down while scrubbing.
- **Camera playback can cause motion sickness.** Mitigation: keyframes store the graph's pose, so playback moves the graph, not you; flights are eased and speed-capped; only forward playback at normal speed applies them, never shuttling or dragging the playhead; the camera track's eye turns them off.
- **Text entry in a headset.** Immersive WebXR offers no system keyboard. Mitigation: an in-world keyboard, dictation where the browser supports speech recognition, and a drawer that can always be browsed without typing.
- **Branches multiply.** Mitigation: two visible at once, only differing tracks doubled, the rest folded in the Branches list with names.
- **Small targets on clips.** Mitigation: clips, edges and rows at least 2.5 deg (about 2.2 cm at 50 cm); the trim scale grows finer as the hand moves away; any value can be typed; ruler zoom when a track gets crowded.
- **Color-only feedback.** Mitigation: diff marks and broken clips carry badges and labels as well as color.

### Comparison row

| #   | Name         | One-line idea                                                                                                                                    | Mode model                                                                    | Navigation                                                                                                          | Selection                                                                                                                    | Command surface                                                                                                                               | Parameters                                                                           | Undo                                                                                                     |
| --- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 23  | Cutting Room | A film editor's timeline under the graph holds every change as a clip on a track; edit the history, not the state, and compare runs by branching | edit the history: every action is a clip; changing one replays all later ones | free grab; camera track of keyframed graph poses; shuttle back and forward by off stick or off-hand pinch and slide | In and Out on a ranked strip of nodes, or a span of steps on the ruler; picking nodes or edges gathers into a selection clip | six tracks, one-shot edit tools (razor, ripple delete, slip), clip drawer opened on the object's verbs, source monitor with each clip's verbs | trim a clip's edge over a pop-out scale; knobs on a clip with live replay downstream | Undo takes back the last timeline edit; older mistakes fixed in place; fork to compare, with a diff lane |

## Patterns across the prototypes

### Inputs that recur

- **Pinch and a ray are the floor.** Every prototype that uses hands can fall back to pointing and
  pinching, and on Apple Vision Pro most of them aim with look-and-pinch. The new ideas sit on top
  of that floor, never in place of it.
- **A work surface low and close, with the graph as the display.** Most of the newer prototypes
  move the hands off the graph onto a near surface at elbow or lap height: the slate in
  Proofreader's Slate (16), the desk in Mixing Desk (21), the forearm grid in Command Card (22),
  the timeline in Cutting Room (23), the rule strip in Show Me (19), the ledge in Hand of Cards
  (13), the rack in Physics Lab (15) and the lap-height whole-graph copy in Voodoo Dolls (14).
  Three of them (16, 21, 22) are built to rest on a real surface -- a table, a lap board, your own
  forearm -- for real touch. This answers arm fatigue and jittery mid-air aim together, and it is
  the strongest single trend in the set.
- **Preview before commit is now the default.** Lean In (12) counts what a drag will change before
  release, Hand of Cards (13) ghosts a card's effect over its target, Voodoo Dolls (14) lets motions
  only propose, Orbits (17) holds every change pending, Linked Views (18) previews drops, Show Me
  (19) is built on a previewed guess, Pilot and Navigator (20) ghosts the token on the target,
  Command Card (22) previews on hover and Cutting Room (23) counts what an edit will replay. A
  mock-up that commits without a preview now needs a reason.
- **The graph moves; the viewer never does.** Physics Lab, Orbits, Pilot and Navigator, Mixing
  Desk, Command Card and Cutting Room all move the graph as an object against the solid background
  and never move the viewpoint, which keeps motion sickness low without a comfort setting.
- **Voice only fills text.** In nearly every new prototype voice is optional and limited to a note,
  a search field or an exact number. Only Point and Say (2) uses it as the command channel, and
  Orbits (17) and Command Card (22) offer it as an optional shortcut to name a tile or cell.
- **Precision by moving the hand away.** One trick appears again and again for fine values:
  slow drags lower the gain in Lean In (12); sliding the nib away from the cut line in
  Proofreader's Slate (16); lifting the hand off the desk in Mixing Desk (21); moving away from the
  pop-out scale in Cutting Room (23); pulling the hand back on a histogram brush in Linked Views
  (18). Combined with detents and a held-value keypad for exact numbers, it covers parameter entry
  without a new gesture.
- **Undo on the object, not on a stack.** Many of the new prototypes make undo selective: undo
  scoped to the object in Lean In (12), lifting a card off its object in Hand of Cards (13),
  throwing a copy away in Voodoo Dolls (14), striking a reading in Proofreader's Slate (16), a
  counter-example in Show Me (19), and editing an old clip in place in Cutting Room (23). All of
  them meet the same problem -- later steps that depended on the undone one -- and all answer it
  the same way: show the dependents before the undo lands, and make the undo itself reversible.
- **The off hand never pinches with its palm toward you.** Several designs (14, 22) found that
  this is the system menu gesture on Quest and Galaxy XR and brings up Home on Vision Pro, and no
  web page can block it. Any off-hand surface has to work without it.

### Combinations that look strongest

- **Linked Views (18) selection with Mixing Desk (21) or Command Card (22) commands.** Selecting in
  histograms and tables avoids the hardest part of graph XR -- picking among many small, occluded
  nodes -- and either a live desk or a fixed forearm grid gives the verbs a stable home.
- **Show Me (19) as a layer over any scheme.** Demonstrate-then-confirm needs only pinch and a
  rule strip, so it can sit beside Context Menus (11), Hand of Cards (13) or Command Card (22) and
  take over whenever the user knows the outcome but not the name of the verb.
- **Hand of Cards (13) records with a Cutting Room (23) timeline.** The same history shown two
  ways: on each object (what was done to this node) and in order (what was done when). Both
  already support out-of-order undo.
- **Proofreader's Slate (16) or Voodoo Dolls (14) for picking, the big graph for looking.** Both
  give an unoccluded, close copy to act on while the full-size graph stays put, and both cite the
  same evidence that touch on a near object beats aiming at a far one.
- **Orbits (17) as the hands-free mode of any scheme.** Look, follow a dot and nod can mirror any
  ring or menu of verbs, which makes it an accessibility layer, not only a stand-alone design.
- **Lean In (12) handles on the graph with a near surface for everything else.** Handles on the
  object are the fastest path for the commonest verbs (neighbors, size, filter by value); the deep
  catalog then lives on a surface.

### Which family handles which hard problem best

The 23 prototypes fall into a handful of families: hand-held or docked command surfaces (1, 5, 11,
21, 22), gesture and sign vocabularies (3, 7, 9), tools and physical objects (4, 6, 8, 15),
controls on the objects themselves (12, 13, 14), language (2, 16), data views and inference (18,
19), gaze (10, 17), history (23) and two people (20).

- **Text.** Language does best: Proofreader's Slate (16) writes words in the margin with
  completion and works with a stylus, a controller or a fingertip; Point and Say (2) speaks; the
  Workbench (5) keyboard types. Gaze (17) and gesture vocabularies (3, 7, 9) are worst. Every
  other family pushes long text to dictation or to the 2D page.
- **Many targets (dense graphs, many nodes).** Data views and inference do best: Linked Views (18)
  selects in histograms and tables, where density does not matter, and Show Me (19) selects by
  rule from examples. Proofreader's Slate (16) helps by flattening the graph so nothing is
  occluded, and Command Card (22) by box select plus stored selection groups. Controls on the
  objects (12, 13) and physical objects (15) suffer most: they need nodes farther apart than
  hand-tracking jitter, or motion that stays readable.
- **Deep catalogs (60+ algorithms and plugins).** Inference and data views again: Show Me (19)
  ranks algorithms by the outcome you showed it, so you never browse; Linked Views (18) makes the
  catalog a table you brush by category, return type and cost. Voodoo Dolls (14) places each
  algorithm by its inputs, so only fitting verbs appear. Proofreader's Slate (16) names any verb as
  a word with completion. Command Card (22) gives every algorithm a permanent address that experts
  learn by feel, at the cost of paging. Gaze (17) and handle-based schemes (12) are slowest, with a
  practical ceiling of about 120 entries for a pull-out list.

## Suggested next steps

Mock these first, in this order:

1. **Linked Views (18).** It has the strongest evidence behind it (brushing and linking, dynamic
   queries, Tableau's shelves), it answers both many targets and deep catalogs, and its views are
   mostly flat panels, so it is cheap to mock. The question to test: do people who came for the
   graph accept it as one view among several?
2. **Command Card (22).** Controllers first, fixed positions, a real surface under the finger. It
   tests the body-anchored fixed layout that several other designs lean on, and its biggest risk
   -- whether the grid stays on an untracked forearm -- is quick to check with one test build.
3. **Show Me (19).** The highest payoff for the catalog problem, and it can run as a
   Wizard-of-Oz mock (a person picks the guess) before any inference exists. The question: do
   people read the preview before they accept?
4. **Cutting Room (23) or Hand of Cards (13).** Undo is the weakest part of XR apps today, and
   these two make history something you see and edit. Mock the timeline over a canned replay;
   real replay needs graphty-element to keep a history that can be edited and branched, where
   today it keeps a linear undo stack, and that work belongs in graphty-element.
5. **Orbits (17).** Head pursuits run in the browser on every headset today and need no hands,
   so a mock is cheap; it doubles as the accessibility layer for the others.

Alongside them, three cheap technical checks would settle questions shared by many designs:
placing a work surface on a real table or lap from a palms-flat gesture (16, 21), fine values by
moving the hand away from the control (12, 16, 21, 23), and which off-hand poses trigger system
gestures on each headset (14, 22). Mixing Desk (21) also depends on capabilities graphty-element
does not have yet -- a style layer's strength, a filter's fade, a value blended from two runs --
which belong in graphty-element before the desk can be more than a mock.
